- Zahir-ud-din Muhammad **Babur**, ruler of Kabul, descended from Timur (father) and Genghis Khan (mother).
- Invited by Daulat Khan Lodi and Rana Sanga to unseat the unpopular Sultan Ibrahim Lodi.

### 2. Rise to Power
- Babur captured Lahore and crossed the Indus with 12,000 disciplined mounted cavalry (1526 CE).
- **First Battle of Panipat (April 21, 1526 CE)**:
  - Defeated Ibrahim Lodi through military tactical genius: Field artillery (**Top-o-Tufang**) and flanking maneuvers (**Tulghuma**).
  - Ibrahim Lodi died on battlefield; Babur proclaimed Emperor at Delhi and Agra.

### 3. Political Expansion
- **Battle of Khanwa (1527 CE)**: Decisively routed the massive Rajput confederacy led by **Rana Sanga of Mewar**.
- **Battle of Chanderi (1528 CE)**: Captured Chanderi fortress from Rajput chieftain Medini Rai.
- **Battle of Ghagra (1529 CE)**: Routed allied Afghan forces along the riverbanks of Bihar.
- Succeeded by son **Humayun (1530 CE)**; faced twin threats from Bahadur Shah of Gujarat and Sher Khan Sur.
- Humayun routed at **Battle of Chausa (1539 CE)** and **Battle of Kannauj (1540 CE)**; fled to Persia.
- Humayun recaptured Delhi and Agra in 1555 CE with Persian military assistance from Shah Tahmasp.

### 4. Administration
- Early governance followed Timurid and Mongol steppe traditions; lacked stable institutionalization.
- Humayun divided court administration astrologically into four departments: Earth, Water, Fire, and Air.
- Empire remained fragile military occupation, highly dependent on the personal charisma of the monarch.

### 5. Revenue System
- Distributed plundered treasury gold of Delhi and Agra lavishly to nobles, earning Babur title **Qalandar**.
- Extracted customary land revenues through existing hereditary rural headmen without systematic surveys.

### 6. Military System
- Revolutionary introduction of **matchlocks and wheeled field artillery** into northern Indian warfare.
- Perfected the **Tulghuma** tactic: Concealing fast-circling flanking cavalry on wings to envelop enemy rear.
- Used **Araba** (chained wooden carts) as a protective mobile barricade for frontline cannons.

### 7. Society
- Influx of refined Turko-Persian, Chagatai, and Central Asian noble families into northern India.
- Humayun faced treacherous desertions and court rebellions from his own ambitious brothers (Kamran, Askari, Hindal).

### 8. Economy
- Babur abolished commercial transit octroi duties (*Tamgha*) to encourage trans-Indus caravan trade.
- Economic recovery severely disrupted by endless succession civil wars between Humayun and Afghan chiefs.

### 9. Religion
- Babur declared the Battle of Khanwa a **Jihad** (holy struggle) against Rana Sanga; broke wine cups.
- Avoided systematic persecution; advised son Humayun in his secret testament to protect cow slaughter and respect all faiths.

### 10. Art & Architecture
- Babur introduced the symmetrical Persian geometric terrace garden (**Charbagh**) to India.
- Built **Aram Bagh** (Agra), **Kabuli Bagh Mosque** (Panipat), and **Jami Masjid** (Sambhal).
- Humayun laid the foundation of the second city of Delhi, **Dinpanah** (Sanctuary of Faith).

### 11. Literature
- **Baburnama (Tuzuk-i-Baburi)**: Authored by Babur in **Chagatai Turkish**; masterpiece of candid world autobiographical literature.
- Humayun patronized Persian scholars, astrologers, and founded royal library at Dinpanah.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- Persian ambassadors, Central Asian scholars, and Ottoman artillery gunners (**Ustad Ali Quli**) served in Babur's army.

### 13. Decline
- Babur died prematurely at Agra in 1530 CE, leaving an unsettled, financially exhausted empire.
- Humayun's indecisiveness and military blunders allowed **Sher Shah Sur** to completely expel him from India.
- Humayun died accidentally in 1556 CE after falling down the library stairs of Sher Mandal, Delhi.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Laid the permanent foundational cornerstone of the glorious **Mughal Empire** in India.
- Revolutionized Indian warfare forever through the systematic deployment of field artillery and gunpowder.
- Introduced Persian garden landscapes and candid historical autobiographical writing to Indian court culture.

---

# 13. Sur Empire (Sher Shah Sur)

### 1. Historical Background
- Farid Khan (Sher Shah), an ethnic Afghan of the Sur tribe, was born at Narnaul (Haryana).
- Managed his father Hasan Khan's jagir at Sasaram, gaining practical experience in rural revenue administration.
- Awarded title **"Sher Khan"** by Bahar Khan Lohani of Bihar for slaying a wild tiger single-handed.

### 2. Rise to Power
- Consolidated control over Bihar; defeated the ruler of Bengal at the **Battle of Surajgarh (1534 CE)**.
- Seized the strategic hill fortress of **Chunar** on the Ganga riverbank.
- Routed Mughal Emperor Humayun at **Battle of Chausa (1539 CE)**; assumed royal title **Sher Shah**.
- Smashed Humayun again at **Battle of Kannauj (Bilgram, 1540 CE)**, seizing Delhi and Agra.
- Established the **Second Afghan Empire (1540–1555 CE)**.

### 3. Political Expansion
- Subdued Bengal and reorganized its administration to prevent provincial rebellions (1541 CE).
- Annexed Malwa (1542 CE) and conquered the strategic fortress of **Raisen (1543 CE)**.
- Invaded Marwar; defeated Rajput hero Jaimal and Rathors at the **Battle of Samel (1544 CE)**.
- Famously remarked after Samel: *"For a handful of bajra (millet), I had almost lost the empire of Hindustan."*
- Besieged Kalinjar Fort (Bundelkhand, 1545 CE); died tragically in an accidental gunpowder explosion.

### 4. Administration
- Masterpiece of centralized administrative efficiency; divided empire into **47 Sarkars (Districts)**:
  - *Sarkar Officers*: **Shiqdar-i-Shiqdaran** (Law, Order, Police) and **Munsif-i-Munsifan** (Judicial & Land Revenue).
  - *Pargana Officers*: Divided into Parganas headed by **Shiqdar**, **Munsif**, **Fotadar** (Treasurer), and **Karkuns** (Scribes).
- Maintained direct royal surveillance; routinely transferred administrative officers every two to three years.
- Impartial Justice: Held court daily; declared justice was the highest religious duty of a sovereign.
- Enforced local community responsibility: Village headmen (**Muqaddams**) were hanged if they failed to catch local thieves.

### 5. Revenue System (The Zabt Prototype)
- Surveyed all cultivated agricultural land using the standardized **Jarib** (bamboo rod with iron rings).
- Prepared official schedule of standard crop yield rates called the **Ray**.
- State share fixed strictly at **one-third (33.3%) of average produce**, payable in cash or kind.
- Issued formal land title deeds (**Patta**) and secured written acceptance deeds (**Qabuliyat**) from peasants.
- Levied minor cesses: *Jaribana* (survey fee 2.5%) and *Muhasilana* (collection fee 5%).

### 6. Military System
- Abolished the undisciplined tribal levy system; built a large **centralized standing army paid in cash**.
- Revived Alauddin Khalji's strict checks: **Dagh** (branding of war-horses) and **Chehra** (descriptive troop rolls).
- Maintained 150,000 cavalry, 25,000 infantry, and 5,000 war-elephants stationed in strategic forts.

### 7. Society
- Enforced rigorous public security; travelers and merchants slept safely in roadside rest-houses without fear.
- Abbas Khan Sarwani recorded: *"An old woman with a basket of gold ornaments could travel safely without guards."*

### 8. Economy
- **The Currency Revolution of Sher Shah**:
  - Abolished debased mixed-metal coinage; created the standard tri-metallic currency of India:
  - Introduced pure silver coin: **Rupiya** (178 grains of pure silver; precursor of modern Indian Rupee).
  - Introduced standard copper coin: **Dam** (380 grains; 40 dams equaled 1 Rupiya).
  - Introduced standard gold coin: **Mohur** (169 grains).
- **The Grand Trunk Road & The 1,700 Sarais**:
  - Reconstructed the ancient **Grand Trunk Road (Sadak-i-Azam)** from Sonargaon (Bengal) to Attock (Indus).
  - Built three additional trunk roads linking Agra with Jodhpur, Burhanpur, and Lahore.
  - Constructed over **1,700 Sarais** (inns) at every two *Krohs* (approx. 4 miles) with separate Hindu/Muslim quarters.
  - Sarais operated as royal postal stations (**Dak Chauki**), ensuring rapid intelligence transmission.

### 9. Religion
- Maintained personal orthodoxy but pursued tolerant, secular administrative policies toward non-Muslim subjects.
- Employed talented Hindu generals, most notably **Brahmajit Gaur**, in the highest military commands.

### 10. Art & Architecture
- Transitioned architecture from Delhi Sultanate rubble into refined classical Mughal proportions.
- **Tomb of Sher Shah at Sasaram (Bihar)**: Magnificent octagonal red-sandstone mausoleum standing inside an artificial lake.
- **Qila-i-Kuhna Mosque (Purana Qila, Delhi)**: Highlights refined pointed arches, marble inlays, and geometric recessed niches.
- **Rohtas Fort (Jhelum, Pakistan)**: Colossal defensive stone fortress built to check rebellious Gakhar tribes.

### 11. Literature
- Commissioned administrative chronicles; patronized Persian, Arabic, and Hindi scholars.
- Malik Muhammad Jayasi composed the celebrated Awadhi epic poem **Padmavat (1540 CE)** during Sher Shah's reign.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- Central Asian merchants and Portuguese travelers marveled at the speed and security of Sher Shah's highways.

### 13. Decline
- Succeeded by son Islam Shah Sur (1545–1554 CE), who maintained order through severe codification of laws.
- Islam Shah's death triggered violent succession civil wars among incompetent Sur princes.
- Humayun invaded from Kabul, defeated Sikandar Sur at Battle of Sirhind (1555 CE), restoring Mughal rule.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Provided the administrative, agrarian, and monetary blueprint that Emperor **Akbar perfected and built upon**.
- Invented the modern **Rupiya** currency, which remains the national monetary unit of India today.
- Reconstructed the Grand Trunk Road, the economic and military arterial lifeline of northern India for centuries.

---

# 14. Mughal Empire: Akbar the Great (1556–1605 CE)

### 1. Historical Background
- Humayun died suddenly in 1556 CE, leaving a fragile, contested kingdom.
- Afghan general Hemu captured Delhi and Agra, proclaiming himself *Raja Vikramaditya*.
- 13-year-old Jalaluddin Muhammad Akbar ascended the throne under regent Bairam Khan.

### 2. Rise to Power
- **Second Battle of Panipat (November 1556 CE)**: Bairam Khan defeated and executed Hemu.
- Ended Bairam Khan's regency (1560 CE) and subsequent Petticoat government (1562 CE).
- Assumed direct autocratic personal command of the Mughal imperial apparatus.

### 3. Political Expansion
- Annexed Malwa (1561 CE), Gondwana under Rani Durgavati (1564 CE), and Gujarat (1572–1573 CE).
- Captured formidable Rajput fortresses of **Chittor (1568 CE)** and **Ranthambore (1569 CE)**.
- Annexed Bengal and Bihar (1576 CE) following the defeat of Daud Khan Karrani.
- **Battle of Haldighati (1576 CE)**: Raja Man Singh defeated Maharana Pratap of Mewar.
- Conquered northern and western frontiers: Kashmir (1586), Sindh (1591), Baluchistan, and Kandahar (1595 CE).
- Southern Deccan campaigns annexed Khandesh, Berar, and parts of Ahmadnagar (1601 CE).

### 4. Administration
- Institutionalized the **Mansabdari System (1571 CE)**:
  - Universal administrative-military framework grading all civil and military officers into a single hierarchy.
  - Dual rank structure: **Zat** (determined personal status and salary) and **Sawar** (cavalry troop quota).
- Imperial territories organized into **15 Subahs (Provinces)** governed by a *Subahdar*.
- Provincial checks and balances: Provincial *Diwan* (Finance) was appointed independently by central *Wazir*.
- Districts (*Sarkars*) managed by *Faujdar* (Executive/Police) and *Amalguzar* (Revenue Collector).
- Cities managed by **Kotwal** (Chief of Police, market superintendent, and municipal registrar).

### 5. Revenue System
- Promulgated the **Ain-i-Dahsala / Zabt System (1580 CE)** designed by **Raja Todar Mal**.
- Measured arable land using the **Tanab** (bamboo rod linked with iron rings).
- Calculated 10-year rolling average of crop yields and prices; state share was **one-third (33.3%)**.
- Classified agricultural land into four soil categories:
  1. *Polaj*: Cultivated continuously every single year.
  2. *Parauti*: Left fallow temporarily for a year or two.
  3. *Chachar*: Left uncultivated for three to four years.
  4. *Banjar*: Wasteland left uncultivated for five or more continuous years.

### 6. Military System
- Enforced strict military muster inspections: **Dagh** (horse branding) and **Chehra** (descriptive troop rolls).
- Maintained diverse military arms: Cavalry, infantry (*Dakhili*), artillery, war-elephants, and river fleets.
- Promoted talented Rajput warrior clans into supreme imperial military commands (*Raja Man Singh*).

### 7. Society
- Rajput diplomatic alliances: Married princesses of Amber, Bikaner, and Jaisalmer without forced conversions.
- Prohibited forcible **Sati** if against the widow's free will; legalized widow remarriage.
- Discouraged child marriage; fixed minimum marriage ages at 16 for boys and 14 for girls.

### 8. Economy
- Standardized currency based on pure silver **Rupiya** and copper **Dam**.
- Agricultural expansion supported by state loans and irrigation encouragements.
- Thriving textile exports of cotton, calicos, silk, and indigo to Europe and West Asia.

### 9. Religion
- Abolished discriminatory taxes: **Pilgrimage Tax (1563 CE)** and **Jizya (1564 CE)**.
- Established **Ibadat Khana (1575 CE)** at Fatehpur Sikri for inter-faith philosophical debates.
- Issued **Mahzar Decree (1579 CE)**: Proclaimed Akbar as **Imam-i-Adil** (Supreme Arbiter) in ambiguous disputes.
- Promulgated **Tauhid-i-Ilahi (Din-i-Ilahi, 1582 CE)**: Ethical spiritual fellowship based on **Sulh-i-Kul (Universal Peace)**.

### 10. Art & Architecture
- Blended Persian, Central Asian, and indigenous Hindu stone architectural traditions.
- **Humayun's Tomb (Delhi)**: First classical Mughal garden-tomb with double dome.
- **Fatehpur Sikri**: Masterpiece capital complex: **Buland Darwaza**, Panch Mahal, Diwan-i-Khas, Salim Chishti Tomb.
- Constructed massive strategic red sandstone fortresses at Agra, Lahore, and Prayagraj (Allahabad).

### 11. Literature
- **Akbarnama & Ain-i-Akbari** by **Abul Fazl**: Monumental court history and administrative gazetteer.
- **Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh** by **Abdul Qadir Badauni**: Critical contemporary orthodox history.
- Established the **Maktab Khana** (Translation Bureau): Translated Mahabharata (*Razmnama*), Ramayana, and Rajatarangini into Persian.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- **Ralph Fitch** (English, 1583–1591 CE): Described Agra and Fatehpur Sikri as far larger than contemporary London.
- Jesuit missionaries (Father Anthony Monserrate and Rodolfo Acquaviva) documented Ibadat Khana debates.

### 13. Decline
- Succession disputes cast a shadow over late reign; Prince Salim (Jahangir) rebelled (1600–1602 CE).
- Salim had Akbar's chief minister and biographer **Abul Fazl assassinated** by Bir Singh Bundela.
- Akbar passed away at Agra in October 1605 CE; buried at Sikandra.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Consolidated the Mughal Empire into a formidable, unified, pan-Indian political and economic powerhouse.
- Formulated the secular, inclusive state policy of **Sulh-i-Kul**, providing enduring ideological stability.
- Created the Mansabdari and Dahsala systems, the administrative backbone of India for over a century.

---

# 15. Mughal Empire: Jahangir & Shah Jahan (1605–1658 CE)

### 1. Historical Background
- Peaceful succession of Prince Salim as **Jahangir** (1605 CE), inheriting a stable, affluent empire.
- Succeeded in 1628 CE by Prince Khurram as **Shah Jahan**, inaugurating imperial architectural zenith.
- Marked the high noon of Mughal court luxury, fine arts, and international commercial diplomacy.

### 2. Rise to Power
- Jahangir crushed the rebellion of his eldest son Prince Khusrau (1606 CE).
- Shah Jahan seized the throne in 1628 CE, eliminating rival dynastic claimants with Asaf Khan's support.

### 3. Political Expansion
- Jahangir concluded the **Treaty of Mewar (1615 CE)** with Rana Amar Singh, ending 50-year conflict.
- Conquered the impregnable mountain fortress of **Kangra (1620 CE)**.
- Safavid Persian Empire recaptured strategic border fortress of **Kandahar (1622 CE)** from Jahangir.
- Shah Jahan annexed **Ahmadnagar Sultanate (1636 CE)**; forced Bijapur and Golconda to accept vassal treaties.
- Launched expensive, failed Central Asian military expeditions to Balkh and Badakhshan (1646–1647 CE).

### 4. Administration
- Jahangir erected the famous **Chain of Justice (Zanjir-i-Adl)** at Agra fort for direct public petitions.
- Introduced **Du-aspa Sih-aspa** rank in Mansabdari: Doubled cavalry quotas without increasing personal Zat salary.
- **The Nur Jahan Junta (1611–1627 CE)**:
  - Empress Nur Jahan, father Itimad-ud-Daula, and brother Asaf Khan dominated court appointments.
  - Coins were struck bearing Nur Jahan's name alongside the Emperor.
- Shah Jahan maintained a highly centralized bureaucracy managed by talented Prime Ministers (Sadullah Khan).

### 5. Revenue System
- Agricultural revenues reached historic peak of 220 million rupees annually under Shah Jahan.
- Lavish state expenditures on monumental palaces, gardens, and imperial display strained finances.
- Gujarat and Deccan suffered a catastrophic, devastating famine (1630–1632 CE); state organized soup kitchens.

### 6. Military System
- Expanded artillery and cavalry forces; failed repeatedly in three sieges to recover Kandahar (1649–1653 CE).
- Increased induction of Deccani nobles and Maratha sardars (Shahji Bhonsle) into Mughal military service.

### 7. Society
- Period of supreme court luxury, fine muslin fabrics, perfumery, and refined cosmopolitan aristocratic etiquette.
- European traveler Bernier observed stark economic disparities between fabulously rich nobles and impoverished peasantry.

### 8. Economy
- Surat flourished as the premier maritime emporium of the Mughal Empire (**Bandar Mubarak**).
- European East India companies (English, Dutch) established thriving coastal factories for textile exports.
- **The Peacock Throne (Takht-i-Taus)**: Encrusted with millions in precious gems and the **Koh-i-Noor** diamond.

### 9. Religion
- Jahangir executed fifth Sikh Guru **Arjan Dev (1606 CE)** for alleged treason, alienating Sikh community.
- Shah Jahan adopted slightly more orthodox policies, destroying newly built Hindu temples in Varanasi.
- **Prince Dara Shikoh** championed profound syncretism, translating 50 Upanishads into Persian (**Sirr-i-Akbar**).

### 10. Art & Architecture
- **Painting Zenith under Jahangir**:
  - Shifted toward refined naturalism, psychological portraits, and European-inspired halos.
  - Master nature artist: **Ustad Mansur** (painted Siberian crane, dodo); master portraitist: **Abul Hasan**.
- **Architecture Zenith under Shah Jahan**:
  - Replaced red sandstone with glowing **White Makrana Marble** and delicate **Pietra Dura** gem inlays.
  - **Taj Mahal (Agra, 1631–1648 CE)**: Mausoleum for Mumtaz Mahal; chief architect **Ustad Ahmad Lahori**.
  - **Shahjahanabad (Delhi, 1638 CE)**: Built the **Red Fort (Lal Qila)** and the colossal **Jama Masjid**.
  - **Tomb of Itimad-ud-Daula (Agra)**: Built by Nur Jahan; exquisite marble jewel-box architecture.

### 11. Literature
- **Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri**: Brilliant, candid autobiographical memoirs penned by Jahangir himself.
- **Padshahnama** by **Abdul Hamid Lahori**: Official court chronicle of Shah Jahan's glorious reign.
- **Majma-ul-Bahrain** by **Dara Shikoh**: Philosophical treatise proving unity of Hinduism and Islam.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- **Captain William Hawkins** (1608 CE) & **Sir Thomas Roe** (1615–1618 CE): Visited Jahangir's imperial court.
- **Francois Bernier** & **Jean-Baptiste Tavernier**: Visited Shah Jahan's court; described Peacock Throne and diamond mines.
- **Niccolao Manucci**: Italian traveler and artilleryman serving Prince Dara Shikoh.

### 13. Decline
- Shah Jahan fell severely ill in September 1657 CE, sparking the **War of Succession**.
- Aurangzeb defeated Dara Shikoh at **Battle of Samugarh (1658 CE)** and **Battle of Deorai (1659 CE)**.
- Aurangzeb imprisoned his aging father Shah Jahan inside Agra Fort, seizing the throne.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Marked the cultural and artistic golden age of Mughal civilization, producing world-renowned architectural wonders.
- Jahangiri miniature painting reached the undisputed aesthetic zenith of South Asian visual art.
- The tragic defeat of liberal Dara Shikoh altered the course of Indian history toward religious polarization.

---

# 16. Mughal Empire: Aurangzeb & Imperial Decline (1658–1707 CE)

### 1. Historical Background
- Aurangzeb Alamgir triumphed in the bloody War of Succession, executing brothers Dara, Shuja, and Murad.
- Inherited a sprawling, wealthy empire facing mounting agrarian strains and regional assertiveness.
- Ruled for 49 years (1658–1707 CE), completely transforming the ideological direction of the state.

### 2. Rise to Power
- Crowned emperor at Delhi in July 1658 CE; adopted imperial title **Alamgir (Conqueror of the World)**.
- Consolidated authority through strict personal puritanism, earning the popular moniker **"Zinda Pir"** (Living Saint).

### 3. Political Expansion
- Extended northeastern frontiers: Mir Jumla conquered **Assam (1662 CE)**; Shaista Khan captured Chittagong (1666 CE).
- **The 25-Year Deccan Campaign (1681–1707 CE)**:
  - Personally shifted entire imperial court and army to Deccan to suppress Marathas.
  - Annexed **Sultanate of Bijapur (1686 CE)** and **Sultanate of Golconda (1687 CE)**.
  - Captured and brutally executed Maratha king **Chhatrapati Sambhaji (1689 CE)**.
- Territorial extent reached all-time maximum: Spanned 21 Subahs from Kabul to Jinji.

### 4. Administration
- Enforced strict Islamic orthodoxy: Banned court music, royal astrology, **Jharokha Darshan**, and **Tula-Dan**.
- Discontinued stamping Islamic Kalma on coins to prevent desecration; banned Persian festival **Nauroz**.
- Appointed **Muhtasibs** (Censors of Public Morals) in major cities to suppress alcohol, gambling, and vice.
- **The Jagirdari Crisis**: Shortage of crown lands (*Be-Jagiri*) to assign to bloated influx of Deccani nobles.

### 5. Revenue System
- Increased imperial revenue demands to 50% or more to finance endless Deccan military campaigns.
- Frequent transfer of Jagirdars led to ruthless, short-sighted over-exploitation of peasant cultivators.
- **The Agrarian Crisis (Irfan Habib Thesis)**: Crushing land revenue sparked widespread armed peasant uprisings.

### 6. Military System
- Maintained the largest standing military force in Mughal history, encamped in the Deccan.
- Incapable of countering swift, mobile guerrilla tactics (**Ganimi Kava**) of Maratha hill cavalry.
- Influx of Maratha and Deccani officers increased Hindu share in Mansabdari to historic peak (**33%**).

### 7. Society & Rebellions
- Series of powerful peasant and regional rebellions:
  - **Jat Peasant Rebellion (Mathura)**: Led by Gokula, Rajaram, and Churaman; desecrated Akbar's tomb.
  - **Satnami Rebellion (1672 CE)**: Armed revolt of lower-caste religious peasants at Narnaul.
  - **Rajput Rebellion (1679 CE)**: Mewar and Marwar revolted over Aurangzeb's interference in Rathor succession.
  - **Sikh Transformation**: Guru Gobind Singh created martial **Khalsa Panth (1699 CE)** against Mughal tyranny.

### 8. Economy
- 25 years of continuous Deccan warfare completely drained the massive imperial treasuries of Shah Jahan.
- Administrative neglect in northern provinces disrupted trade routes, river transport, and agriculture.

### 9. Religion
- Completely reversed Akbar's policy of *Sulh-i-Kul*; ruled strictly according to orthodox Hanafi Sharia.
- Ordered destruction of prominent Hindu temples (Kashi Vishwanath at Varanasi, Keshavdeva at Mathura).
- Executed ninth Sikh Guru **Tegh Bahadur (1675 CE)** for defending Kashmiri Pandits.
- **Re-imposed Jizya (1679 CE)** after 115 years of abolition, alienating millions of Hindu subjects.

### 10. Art & Architecture
- Dispersed court painters and musicians; artistic patronage declined sharply at Delhi.
- **Bibi Ka Maqbara (Aurangabad, 1660 CE)**: Built for Queen Dilras Banu Begum; known as "Poor Man's Taj".
- **Badshahi Mosque (Lahore, 1673 CE)**: Monumental red-sandstone mosque with white marble domes.
- **Moti Masjid (Delhi Red Fort)**: Small white marble private prayer mosque for Aurangzeb.

### 11. Literature
- **Fatawa-i-Alamgiri**: Monumental encyclopedic compendium of Islamic jurisprudence compiled by royal scholars.
- **Muntakhab-ul-Lubab** by **Khafi Khan**: Candid, clandestine history recording peasant distress and Maratha wars.
- **Ma'asir-i-Alamgiri** by **Saqi Mustaid Khan**: Detailed official posthumous biography of Aurangzeb.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- **Niccolao Manucci**: Documented Aurangzeb's court intrigues, Deccan wars, and religious measures in *Storia do Mogor*.
- English and French East India Company factors documented Mughal naval skirmishes (Child's War 1686–1690 CE).

### 13. Decline
- The **"Deccan Ulcer"** ruined Aurangzeb physically, militarily, and financially:
  > *"Just as the Spanish ulcer ruined Napoleon, the Deccan ulcer ruined Aurangzeb."*
- Died exhausted at Ahmadnagar in March 1707 CE at age 88; buried in a simple open-air grave at Khuldabad.
- Imperial authority disintegrated rapidly under weak successors, triggering 18th-century fragmentation.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Represented the ultimate territorial zenith and simultaneous structural exhaustion of the Mughal Empire.
- Religious polarization and alienation of Rajputs, Sikhs, and Marathas shattered the imperial social fabric.
- Paved the way for the rise of the **Maratha Confederacy** and eventual British colonial conquest of India.

---

# 17. The Maratha Empire & The Peshwas (1627–1761 CE)

### 1. Historical Background
- Rugged topography of the Western Ghats (Sahyadris) fostered fiercely independent martial peasant communities.
- Bhakti saints (Jnaneshwar, Namdev, Eknath, Tukaram, Ramdas) created a shared democratic Marathi cultural identity.
- Maratha chieftains served as seasoned military cavalry commanders under the Deccan Sultanates.

### 2. Rise to Power
- **Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj (1627–1680 CE)**: Born at Shivneri Fort to Shahji Bhonsle and Jijabai.
- Captured Torna Fort at age 16; built an independent kingdom (**Hindavi Swarajya**).
- Slew Bijapuri general **Afzal Khan** using tiger claws (*Bagh Nakh*) at Pratapgad (1659 CE).
- Conducted daring midnight raid on Mughal viceroy **Shaista Khan** inside Pune (1663 CE).
- Sacked rich Mughal port city of **Surat (1664 CE)**, humiliating imperial authority.
- Signed **Treaty of Purandar (1665 CE)** with Raja Jai Singh; surrendered 23 forts; visited Agra court (1666 CE).
- Staged daring escape from Agra inside sweet baskets; recaptured all lost fortresses.
- Grand coronation as **Chhatrapati at Raigad Fort (1674 CE)**; officiated by Gaga Bhatta of Varanasi.

### 3. Political Expansion
- Shivaji conducted southern campaigns (1677–1678 CE), capturing Jinji and Vellore in Tamil Nadu.
- Succeeded by Chhatrapati Sambhaji (1680–1689 CE), who fought Mughals relentlessly until captured and martyred.
- **Maratha War of Independence (1689–1707 CE)**: Led by Rajaram, Santaji Ghorpade, Dhanaji Jadhav, and **Queen Tarabai**.
- **The Golden Age of the Peshwas (1713–1761 CE)**:
  - *Balaji Vishwanath (1713–1720 CE)*: Secured Mughal sanad for Chauth and Sardeshmukhi across six Deccan provinces.
  - *Peshwa Baji Rao I (1720–1740 CE)*: Military genius; proclaimed **Hindu-Pad-Padshahi**; expanded Marathas to Delhi.
  - Famously declared: *"Let us strike at the trunk of the withered tree, and the branches will fall of themselves."*
  - Defeated Nizam at **Battle of Palkhed (1728 CE)**; conquered Malwa, Gujarat, and Bundelkhand.
  - *Peshwa Balaji Baji Rao (Nana Saheb, 1740–1761 CE)*: Expanded empire from Attock (Indus) to Cuttack.

### 4. Administration
- Shivaji's Council of Eight Ministers: **Ashta Pradhan** (purely advisory; non-hereditary):
  1. *Peshwa (Mukhya Pradhan)*: Prime Minister; general administration.
  2. *Amatya (Majumdar)*: Finance Minister; audited accounts.
  3. *Sachiv (Shurnavis)*: Royal correspondence; edicts.
  4. *Mantri (Waqia-Navis)*: Royal household intelligence.
  5. *Senapati (Sari-Naubat)*: Commander-in-Chief of army.
  6. *Sumant (Dabir)*: Foreign Minister.
  7. *Nyayadhish*: Chief Justice.
  8. *Panditrao*: Religious affairs and charities.
- Under later Peshwas, administration decentralized into a **Maratha Confederacy**:
  - *Gaekwads of Baroda, Scindias of Gwalior, Holkars of Indore, Bhonsles of Nagpur, Peshwas of Pune*.

### 5. Revenue System
- Measured land directly using the **Kathi** measuring rod; eliminated corrupt zamindari intermediaries.
- Fixed land revenue at **40% of produce** after abolishing all traditional illegal cesses.
- **Chauth**: Military protection tax amounting to **one-fourth (25%) of standard land revenue** from foreign territories.
- **Sardeshmukhi**: Additional hereditary levy of **one-tenth (10%)** claimed by Shivaji as supreme head of Maharashtra.

### 6. Military System
- Mastered **Ganimi Kava** (guerrilla hit-and-run mountain warfare utilizing steep Sahyadri terrain).
- Dual Cavalry Force:
  - *Bargirs*: Regular state-equipped cavalry troopers mounted on royal horses.
  - *Silahdars*: Feudal noble cavalry supplying their own horses and armaments.
- Maintained formidable defensive hill forts and an independent naval fleet commanded by **Kanhoji Angre**.

### 7. Society
- Egalitarian social mobilization: United diverse Marathi castes (Marathas, Kunbis, Brahmans, Mahars).
- Relied on mountain hill-tribesmen (**Mavalas**) as elite infantry and fortress guardians.

### 8. Economy
- Extracted vast annual Chauth and Sardeshmukhi tributes from across Mughal India.
- Pune, Satara, and Nagpur developed into flourishing banking and artisanal trade metropolises.

### 9. Religion
- Proclaimed **Hindavi Swarajya** and protection of cows, Brahmanas, and sacred shrines.
- Maintained profound tolerance: Respected Quran, protected Muslim women, and employed Muslim naval captains.
- Spiritual inspiration drawn from Bhakti saints **Sant Tukaram** and **Samarth Ramdas**.

### 10. Art & Architecture
- Superb mountain military engineering: Over 300 hill forts with double walls and cisterns carved into living rock.
- Sea fortresses: **Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg, and Suvarnadurg**.
- Promoted traditional wooden residential palace architecture (**Wadas**) in Pune.

### 11. Literature
- Promoted Marathi language and **Modi script** for official administrative documents and revenue registers.
- Commissioned *Rajyavyavahara Kosha*: Specialized glossary replacing Persian terms with Sanskrit administrative equivalents.

### 12. Foreign Travellers
- English factory factors at Surat and Bombay documented Shivaji's daring raids and military brilliance.
- Portuguese records at Goa documented Maratha naval supremacy under Kanhoji Angre.

### 13. Decline
- Maratha Confederacy suffered internal factional rivalries among Scindias, Holkars, and Peshwas.
- Over-extended into northern India, alienating potential allies: Rajputs, Jats, and Awadh.
- **Third Battle of Panipat (January 14, 1761 CE)**:
  - Afghan invader **Ahmad Shah Abdali** crushed the Marathas commanded by Sadashivrao Bhau.
  - Decimated 28,000 elite Maratha warriors, shattering dreams of a pan-Indian Maratha empire.

### 14. Historical Significance
- Successfully shattered the Mughal imperial hegemony, becoming the foremost political power in 18th-century India.
- Created the finest indigenous guerrilla military tradition and naval coastal defense in early modern Asia.
- The power vacuum left by Panipat (1761 CE) facilitated the rapid territorial expansion of the British East India Company.

---

# 18. Indo-Islamic Art & Architecture

## 🏛️ Chronological Evolution of Indo-Islamic Architecture

```text
Architectural Phases of Medieval India:
  ├── 1. Mamluk / Imperial Delhi Style (1193 – 1290 CE): Quwwat-ul-Islam, Qutb Minar, Balban's tomb (first true arch)
  ├── 2. Khalji Style (1290 – 1320 CE): Alai Darwaza (true dome, horseshoe arch), Siri Fort
  ├── 3. Tughlaq Style (1320 – 1414 CE): Sloping battered walls, grey sandstone, austere rubble masonry
  ├── 4. Sayyid & Lodi Style (1414 – 1526 CE): Introduction of double dome, octagonal and square tombs
  ├── 5. Provincial Styles (14th – 16th Century CE): Sharqi (Jaunpur), Malwa (Mandu), Gujarat, Bengal, Deccan
  └── 6. Mughal Imperial Style (1526 – 1707 CE): Red sandstone, white marble, Charbagh layout, Pietra dura, bulbous domes
```

---

## 🔍 Master Architectural Elements Matrix

- **The Arcuate System**:
  - Replaced the indigenous trabeate (beam-and-lintel) technique with the true **arch and keystone**.
  - Replaced flat stone horizontal roofs with the soaring **true dome**.
- **Mortar Technology**:
  - Use of high-grade lime-surkhi hydraulic mortar enabled massive spans across open interior halls.
- **Decorative Motifs (Absence of Human Figurines)**:
  - Strict adherence to Islamic aniconism; avoided sculpting human and animal idols in religious structures.
  - **Calligraphy**: Elegant Quranic verses incised in Naskh, Kufic, and Nastaliq scripts.
  - **Arabesque**: Continuous, flowing mathematical geometric interlacing of stems, leaves, and tendrils.
  - **Geometric Tessellations**: Hexagons, octagons, and star patterns symbolizing infinite cosmic order.
  - **Jali Screens**: Intricate, pierced stone lattice windows filtering blinding tropical sunlight.
  - **Pietra Dura (Parchin Kari)**: Inlaying polished semi-precious gems (lapis lazuli, carnelian, jasper, jade) into marble.

---

## 🕌 Mughal Architecture Epochs & Landmarks

- **Babur & Humayun**:
  - Babur laid out early geometric terrace gardens (**Charbagh**) at Aram Bagh (Agra) and Kabuli Bagh (Panipat).
  - Humayun built the citadel palace of **Dinpanah** in Delhi.
- **Akbar's Age of Red Sandstone**:
  - **Humayun's Tomb (Delhi)**: Designed by Mirak Mirza Ghiyas; first Mughal Charbagh garden-tomb with double dome.
  - **Fatehpur Sikri City (1571–1585 CE)**: Masterpiece complex: Buland Darwaza, Panch Mahal, Jodha Bai's Palace, Diwan-i-Khas.
  - **Agra Fort & Lahore Fort**: Massive red sandstone defensive palace-fortresses with sandstone brackets.
- **Jahangir's Transitional Phase**:
  - **Tomb of Itimad-ud-Daula (Agra, 1622–1628 CE)**: Built by Nur Jahan; first monument built entirely of white marble with **Pietra Dura**.
  - **Shalimar and Nishat Gardens (Kashmir)**: Terraced formal Mughal water gardens with cascading fountains.
- **Shah Jahan's Golden Age of White Marble**:
  - **Taj Mahal (Agra, 1631–1648 CE)**: Chief architect **Ustad Ahmad Lahori**; pinnacle of Mughal architectural symmetry and grace.
  - **Red Fort of Delhi (1638–1648 CE)**: Red sandstone palace-fortress containing Diwan-i-Aam, Diwan-i-Khas, Rang Mahal, and Nahr-i-Bihisht.
  - **Jama Masjid (Delhi, 1656 CE)**: Colossal congregational mosque featuring three white marble domes and two 130-foot minarets.

---

# 19. Medieval Paintings & Music

## 🎨 Master Schools of Medieval Painting

### 1. The Mughal Imperial Miniature Painting School
- **Origins under Humayun**:
  - Humayun brought master Persian painters **Mir Sayyid Ali** and **Abdus Samad** from Safavid Persia to Kabul and Delhi.
- **Akbar's Royal Painting Studio (Tasvir Khana)**:
  - Established formal imperial atelier managed by Abdus Samad; integrated dozens of talented indigenous Hindu painters.
  - Celebrated Indian painters: **Daswant, Basawan, Kesu, Lal, Mukund, and Tara**.
  - Major Illustrated Manuscripts:
    - *Hamzanama* (Story of Amir Hamza): Massive project spanning 1,400 large paintings on cotton fabric.
    - *Tutinama* (Tales of a Parrot): Early fusion of Persian and Indian folk styles.
    - *Razmnama*: Elaborately illustrated Persian translation of the Hindu epic Mahabharata.
- **Jahangir's Golden Zenith (Naturalism & Portraiture)**:
  - Painting shifted from crowded group narrative compositions to refined, individualized portraiture and nature studies.
  - Master nature and bird artist: **Ustad Mansur** (awarded title *Nadir-ul-Asr*); painted flora, fauna, and rare birds.
  - Master human portraitist: **Abul Hasan** (awarded title *Nadir-uz-Zaman*); painted Jahangir's coronation scene.
  - Master psychological portraitist: **Bishandas**; dispatched to Persia to paint Shah Abbas I.
  - Introduction of the **Halo (Divine Nimbus)** around the head of emperors, inspired by European Christian art.
- **Decline under Aurangzeb**:
  - Court painters dispersed to regional provincial kingdoms: Rajasthan (Mewar, Marwar, Bundi, Kishangarh) and Pahari hills (Kangra, Basohli).

---

## 🎵 Medieval Music & Performing Traditions

- **Amir Khusrau (1253–1325 CE)**:
  - Titled **Tuti-i-Hind (Parrot of India)**; revolutionary pioneer of North Indian classical music.
  - Created the musical genre of **Qawwali** by synthesizing Persian lyric poetry with Indian classical melodies.
  - Credited with introducing musical styles **Khayal, Tarana, and Qalb**.
  - Credited with inventing the stringed **Sitar** (adapting the Veena) and the **Tabla** (adapting the Pakhawaj).
- **The Dhrupad Tradition & Miyan Tansen**:
  - **Gwalior Court of Raja Man Singh Tomar**: Center of the ancient classical **Dhrupad** vocal tradition; compiled the music treatise *Man Kautuhal*.
  - **Miyan Tansen**:
    - Foremost musician at Akbar's imperial court; awarded royal title **Mian** by Akbar.
    - Disciple of the great Vaishnavite saint-musician **Swami Haridas** of Vrindavan.
    - Created celebrated new ragas: **Miyan ki Todi, Miyan ki Malhar, Miyan ki Sarang, and Darbari Kanada**.
- **Royal Music Treatises**:
  - *Gita Govinda* by **Jayadeva** (12th c. Bengal): Celebrated lyrical Sanskrit songs describing love of Radha and Krishna.
  - *Sangita Ratnakara* by **Sarangadeva** (13th c. Yadava court): Authoritative classical musicological text of medieval India.
  - *Tuhfat-ul-Hind* by **Mirza Khan** (reign of Aurangzeb): Encyclopedic Persian work analyzing Indian music theory, grammar, and poetics.

---

# 20. Medieval Literature & Historiography

## 📚 Master Directory of Medieval Historical Chronicles

| Historical Work | Author / Chronicler | Royal Patron / Reign | Language | Core Subject Matter & UPSC Significance |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| **Chachnama** | Ali bin Hamid Kufi (trans.) | c. 1216 CE (from Arabic) | Persian | Detailed contemporary history of Arab conquest of Sindh under Muhammad bin Qasim (712 CE). |
| **Kitab-ul-Hind** | **Al-Biruni** | Mahmud of Ghazni (1030 CE) | Arabic | Masterly, objective encyclopedia on Indian astronomy, philosophy (*Gita*), mathematics, calendar, and society. |
| **Taj-ul-Ma'asir** | **Hasan Nizami** | Qutbuddin Aibak & Iltutmish | Arabic/Persian | First official dynastic chronicle of Delhi Sultanate; covers 1191 to 1217 CE. |
| **Tabaqat-i-Nasiri** | **Minhaj-us-Siraj** | Sultan Nasiruddin Mahmud | Persian | Authoritative universal Islamic history; premier contemporary source for Mamluk dynasty and Mongol invasions. |
| **Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi** | **Ziauddin Barani** | Firoz Shah Tughlaq (1357 CE) | Persian | Masterpiece of medieval Indian historiography; details Balban, Khalji market reforms, and Muhammad Tughlaq. |
| **Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi** | **Shams-i-Siraj Afif** | Firoz Shah Tughlaq | Persian | Detailed, sympathetic chronicle of Firoz Tughlaq's canals, public buildings, departments, and administration. |
| **Futuh-us-Salatin** | **Isami** | Alauddin Bahman Shah (1350) | Persian (Verse) | Metered historical epic covering Ghaznavids to Tughlaqs; critical of Muhammad bin Tughlaq. |
| **Futuhat-i-Firoz Shahi** | **Firoz Shah Tughlaq** | Self-authored memoir | Persian | Brief royal autobiography documenting Firoz Shah's civic charities, canal projects, and religious bans. |
| **Baburnama (Tuzuk)** | **Babur** | Self-authored memoir | **Chagatai Turkish** | Brilliant, candid personal autobiography; details Indian battles, fauna, flora, climate, architecture, and society. |
| **Humayun-nama** | **Gulbadan Begum** | Commissioned by Akbar | Persian | Intimate, domestic historical biography written by Humayun's sister; details royal Mughal harem life. |
| **Tarikh-i-Sher Shahi** | **Abbas Khan Sarwani** | Commissioned by Akbar | Persian | Detailed administrative history of Sher Shah Sur's land reforms, currency, sarais, and military. |
| **Akbarnama** | **Abul Fazl** | Emperor Akbar | Persian | Monumental official three-volume court history; documents Akbar's genealogy, reign, and conquests. |
| **Ain-i-Akbari** | **Abul Fazl** | Emperor Akbar (Vol. 3) | Persian | Statistical gazetteer of Mughal Empire; details Mansabdari, Dahsala revenue system, administrative subahs, and crafts. |
| **Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh**| **Abdul Qadir Badauni** | Reign of Akbar (secret) | Persian | Candid, orthodox critique of Akbar; condemns Din-i-Ilahi and court religious debates. |
| **Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri** | **Jahangir** | Self-authored memoir | Persian | Candid royal memoirs; details judicial chain, justice, royal ordinances, and artistic painting patronage. |
| **Padshahnama** | **Abdul Hamid Lahori** | Emperor Shah Jahan | Persian | Official imperial chronicle of first twenty years of Shah Jahan; detailed accounts of court architecture. |
| **Majma-ul-Bahrain** | **Prince Dara Shikoh** | 1655 CE | Persian | "Mingling of Two Oceans"; philosophical treatise synthesizing Hindu Advaita Vedanta with Sufi Islamic mysticism. |
| **Sirr-i-Akbar** | **Prince Dara Shikoh** | 1657 CE | Persian | "The Great Secret"; landmark Persian translation of 50 classical Upanishads assisted by Banaras pandits. |
| **Alamgirnama** | **Mirza Muhammad Kazim** | Emperor Aurangzeb | Persian | Official court history of first ten years of Aurangzeb's reign; later banned by the emperor. |
| **Muntakhab-ul-Lubab** | **Khafi Khan** | Composed secretly (1730s) | Persian | Clandestine history recording severe peasant distress, agrarian crisis, and Maratha guerrilla warfare under Aurangzeb. |
| **Nuskha-i-Dilkusha** | **Bhimsen Saxena** | Aurangzeb's Deccan Wars | Persian | Eyewitness soldier's memoir; documents military campaigns, Maratha warfare, and Deccan administrative collapse. |

# 21. Medieval Economic Terminology & Institutions

## 📜 Master Glossary of High-Yield Medieval Economic Terms

| Technical Term | Exact Meaning & Administrative Function | Dynastic Period / Context |
|:---|:---|:---|
| **Iqta** | Non-hereditary, transferable revenue assignment granted to military commanders for salary and troops. | Delhi Sultanate |
| **Fawazil** | Surplus land revenue remitted by provincial Muqtis to the central imperial treasury. | Delhi Sultanate |
| **Kharaj** | Standard land revenue tax levied on agricultural produce of non-Muslim peasants (25% to 50%). | Sultanate & Mughal |
| **Jizya** | Poll tax levied on non-Muslim subjects (*Zimmis*) in return for life protection and military exemption. | Sultanate & Mughal |
| **Zakat** | Canonical religious tax (2.5%) paid voluntarily by wealthy Muslims on property and cattle. | Islamic Sharia |
| **Khams** | State share of one-fifth (20%) reserved from spoils of war and mineral mines. | Islamic Sharia |
| **Chari** | Grazing tax levied on pasture lands and milk-giving cattle, introduced by Alauddin Khalji. | Delhi Sultanate |
| **Ghari** | Residential house tax levied on dwelling compounds, introduced by Alauddin Khalji. | Delhi Sultanate |
| **Diwan-i-Amir-Kohi** | Specialized agriculture ministry created by Muhammad bin Tughlaq to promote state farming. | Tughlaq Dynasty |
| **Sondhar / Taccavi**| Low-interest agricultural loans disbursed by the state to distress-hit peasants for buying bullocks and seeds. | Sultanate & Mughal |
| **Karkhanas** | State-owned imperial manufacturing workshops producing textiles, arms, and royal luxury goods. | Sultanate & Mughal |
| **Haqq-i-Sharb** | Canal water irrigation tax (10% of produce) introduced by Firoz Shah Tughlaq. | Tughlaq Dynasty |
| **Gaz-i-Sikandari** | Standardized land measurement yard (32 digits) introduced by Sikandar Lodi. | Lodi Dynasty |
| **Ray** | Official schedule of standard crop yields per bigha, formulated under Sher Shah Sur. | Sur Dynasty |
| **Jarib** | Standardized land measuring chain made of bamboo poles linked with iron rings. | Sur & Mughal |
| **Patta** | Formal land title deed issued by government detailing peasant's land boundaries and tax dues. | Sur Dynasty |
| **Qabuliyat** | Written deed of acceptance signed by peasant agreeing to pay stipulated state revenue. | Sur Dynasty |
| **Rupiya** | Standard high-purity silver coin (178 grains) introduced by Sher Shah Sur. | Sur & Mughal |
| **Dam** | Massive copper coin (380 grains) introduced by Sher Shah Sur; 40 dams equaled 1 Rupiya. | Sur & Mughal |
| **Mansab** | Military-administrative rank grading officers; comprised dual Zat (personal status) and Sawar (troop quota). | Mughal Empire |
| **Du-aspa Sih-aspa** | Mansabdari modification introduced by Jahangir; doubled Sawar trooper quota without increasing Zat salary. | Mughal Empire |
| **Zabt / Dahsala** | Land revenue system based on 10-year average crop yields and prices; state share was 1/3rd. | Mughal Empire (Akbar) |
| **Polaj** | Ideal agricultural land cultivated continuously every year without leaving fallow. | Mughal Empire |
| **Parauti** | Land left fallow temporarily for one or two years to replenish soil fertility. | Mughal Empire |
| **Chachar** | Marginal agricultural land left uncultivated for three to four years. | Mughal Empire |
| **Banjar** | Wasteland left uncultivated for five or more continuous years. | Mughal Empire |
| **Khalisa** | Direct crown agricultural lands whose entire revenues flowed straight to central imperial treasury. | Mughal Empire |
| **Jagir** | Territorial revenue assignment granted to Mansabdars in lieu of cash salaries (*Tankha Jagir*). | Mughal Empire |
| **Paibaqi** | Pool of unassigned crown lands awaiting distribution as jagirs to Mansabdars. | Mughal Empire |
| **Watan Jagir** | Hereditary, non-transferable ancestral homeland estates confirmed to Rajput autonomous chiefs by Mughals. | Mughal Empire |
| **Madad-i-Maash** | Hereditary, tax-free charitable land grant (*Sayurghal*) bestowed upon scholars, saints, and religious institutions. | Mughal Empire |
| **Hundi** | Negotiable financial credit note and bill of exchange issued by merchant bankers (**Sarrafs**). | Mughal Economy |
| **Sarraf** | Indigenous money-changer, bullion tester, and merchant banker handling currency and Hundis. | Mughal Economy |
| **Chauth** | Military protection levy (25% of land revenue) extracted by Marathas from foreign territories. | Maratha Empire |
| **Sardeshmukhi** | Hereditary fiscal levy (additional 10%) claimed by Shivaji as supreme lord of Maharashtra. | Maratha Empire |
| **Bargir** | State-equipped and state-funded regular Maratha cavalry trooper. | Maratha Empire |
| **Silahdar** | Independent Maratha cavalry soldier who supplied and maintained his own horse and weaponry. | Maratha Empire |

# 22. Foreign Travellers in Medieval India

## 🧭 Master Matrix of Foreign Travellers

| Traveler & Origin | Visiting Dates | Ruling Monarch / Dynasty Visited | Travelogue / Book | Key Historical Observations & UPSC Traps |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| **Al-Masudi** (Arab) | 915 – 916 CE | Gurjara-Pratiharas & Rashtrakutas | **Muruj adh-Dhahab** (*Meadows of Gold*) | Described Pratiharas as *Al-Juzr*; praised Rashtrakuta kings as friendly to foreign Arab merchants. |
| **Al-Biruni** (Persian) | 1017 – 1030 CE | Mahmud of Ghazni (Northern India) | **Kitab-ul-Hind** (*Tarikh al-Hind*) | Analyzed Indian philosophy, caste, astronomy; documented eight *Antyaja* untouchable castes living outside villages. |
| **Marco Polo** (Venetian) | 1292 – 1294 CE | Pandya Kingdom (Madurai) | **The Travels of Marco Polo** | Visited Kayal port; described pearl fisheries, maritime trade with China, sati, and horse imports from Arabia. |
| **Ibn Battuta** (Moroccan) | 1333 – 1342 CE | Tughlaq Dynasty (**Muhammad bin Tughlaq**) | **Rihla** (*The Travelogue*) | Served as Chief Qazi of Delhi for 8 years; documented postal runner system (*Dawa* and *Uluq*), sati, and MBT's character. |
| **Nicolo Conti** (Italian) | 1420 – 1421 CE | Vijayanagara Empire (**Devaraya I**) | Travel notes in Latin | Described Hampi's defensive fortifications, currency, slave trade, and grand religious festivals (Diwali, Navaratri). |
| **Abdur Razzaq** (Persian) | 1443 – 1444 CE | Vijayanagara Empire (**Devaraya II**) | **Matla-us-Sadain** | Ambassador of Timurid Shah Rukh; described Hampi as having **seven concentric fortified stone walls**. |
| **Afanasy Nikitin** (Russian) | 1469 – 1472 CE | Bahmani Sultanate (**Muhammad Shah III**) | **The Journey Beyond Three Seas** | Russian merchant; documented Bidar's horse trade, Prime Minister Mahmud Gawan, and stark poverty of peasantry. |
| **Duarte Barbosa** (Portuguese)| 1500 – 1516 CE | Vijayanagara (**Krishnadevaraya**) | **The Book of Duarte Barbosa** | Described commercial wealth of Malabar ports, textile exports, and religious freedom under Krishnadevaraya. |
| **Domingo Paes** (Portuguese) | 1520 – 1522 CE | Vijayanagara (**Krishnadevaraya**) | **Chronica dos Reis de Bisnaga** | Eyewitness at Krishnadevaraya's court; described royal ceremonies, Mahanavami festival, and compared Hampi to Rome. |
| **Fernao Nuniz** (Portuguese) | 1535 – 1537 CE | Vijayanagara (**Achyuta Deva Raya**) | **Chronicle of the Kings of Vijayanagara** | Horse dealer; recorded detailed history of four dynasties, military reviews, court women wrestlers, and Sati. |
| **Ralph Fitch** (English) | 1583 – 1591 CE | Mughal Empire (**Akbar the Great**) | Travel Letters | First Englishman to visit Akbar's court; described Agra and Fatehpur Sikri as larger than London. |
| **William Hawkins** (English) | 1608 – 1611 CE | Mughal Empire (**Jahangir**) | Factory reports | First East India Company envoy; conversed in Turkish with Jahangir; awarded mansab of 400. |
| **Sir Thomas Roe** (English) | 1615 – 1618 CE | Mughal Empire (**Jahangir**) | **The Embassy of Sir Thomas Roe** | Official ambassador of King James I; secured royal Farman for English trading factory at Surat. |
| **Peter Mundy** (English) | 1628 – 1634 CE | Mughal Empire (**Shah Jahan**) | Travel Diaries | Documented the catastrophic Gujarat-Deccan famine of 1630–1632 CE and construction of the Taj Mahal. |
| **Francois Bernier** (French) | 1656 – 1668 CE | Mughal Empire (**Shah Jahan & Aurangzeb**) | **Travels in the Mogul Empire** | Physician to Prince Dara Shikoh; documented War of Succession, karkhanas, and theorized absence of private property. |
| **Jean-Baptiste Tavernier** | 1640 – 1668 CE | Mughal Empire (**Shah Jahan & Aurangzeb**) | **Six Voyages to India** | French gem merchant; visited Golconda diamond mines; provided first detailed description of **Peacock Throne**. |
| **Niccolao Manucci** (Italian) | 1653 – 1708 CE | Mughal Empire (Dara & Aurangzeb) | **Storia do Mogor** (*Mughal India*) | Served as artilleryman to Dara Shikoh; documented four decades of court intrigues, battles, and medicine. |

# 24. Study Plan & PYQ Mastery System

## 🎯 6-Phase Preparation Blueprint for UPSC Medieval History

```text
The 6-Phase Medieval Preparation Strategy:

Phase 1: Chronological Backbone (Days 1–2)
  └── Master chronological sequence from 712 CE to 1761 CE. Memorize dynastic founders, reigns, and major battle dates.

Phase 2: The Two Imperial Core Pillars (Days 3–6)
  ├── 1. Delhi Sultanate: Balban's kingship, Alauddin's market reforms, MBT's 5 experiments, Firoz Tughlaq's canals.
  └── 2. Mughal Empire: Akbar's Mansabdari & Dahsala, Rajput policy, religious trajectory, Shah Jahan's architecture, Aurangzeb's decline.

Phase 3: The Southern Powers (Days 7–8)
  └── Vijayanagara Empire (Nayankara & Ayagar systems, Hampi art) + Deccan Sultanates (Adil Shahis, Golconda) + Chhatrapati Shivaji's Ashta Pradhan.

Phase 4: Socio-Religious Movements (Days 9–10)
  └── Bhakti Movement (Nirguna vs. Saguna, regional saints, female mystics) + Sufi Movement (Chishti, Suhrawardi, Naqshbandi silsilas).

Phase 5: Master Directories & Flashcards (Days 11–12)
  └── Memorize Terminology (Topic 21), Historiography Chronicles (Topic 20), Foreign Travellers (Topic 22), and Architecture (Topic 18).

Phase 6: PYQ Reverse-Engineering & Active Recall (Days 13–14)
  └── Solve last 12 years of Prelims PYQs (2013–2025) and write 10 Mains analytical answers on agrarian and institutional themes.
```

---

## 📊 10-Year UPSC Prelims Trend Analysis (Medieval History)

- **Heavy Weightage on Administrative & Revenue Terminology**:
  - Questions regularly test exact meanings of specialized terms:
    - *Amil / Pargana / Shiqdar* (2019, 2021).
    - *Fanam* (coins — 2022).
    - *Iqta / Wazir / Ariz-i-Mamalik* (2014, 2018).
    - *Mahattara & Pattakila* (village headmen — 2014).
    - *Dahsala / Polaj / Parauti* (2015, 2020).
- **Foreign Travellers & Eyewitness Accounts**:
  - Direct matching of travellers to reigning monarchs: *Domingo Paes, Fernao Nuniz, Duarte Barbosa* (2015, 2021, 2023).
- **Bhakti & Sufi Saint Philosophical Formulations**:
  - Synchronisms of saints with contemporary rulers: *Kabir, Guru Nanak, Chaitanya* (2014, 2019).
  - Philosophical doctrines: *Advaita, Vishishtadvaita, Shuddhadvaita* (2017, 2022).

---

## 🔄 Final Active Recall Protocol

- **Pass 1 (Economic Terms)**: Review Topic 21. Ensure instant recall of terms like *Iqta, Fawazil, Chauth, Sardeshmukhi, Hundi, Polaj, Paibaqi*.
- **Pass 2 (Chronicles & Authors)**: Review Topic 20. Match authors with royal patrons without looking at answers.
- **Pass 3 (Map Coordinates)**: Review Topic 23. Mentally locate all 40 battlefields, forts, and capitals on an outline map of India.

# PART III: MODERN INDIAN HISTORY

# Modern Indian History: The Definitive UPSC Master Framework

> **Reference Standard**: Aligned strictly with NCERT, Bipan Chandra (*Modern India*), Shekhar Bandyopadhyay (*From Plassey to Partition*), Spectrum, and UPSC CSE Syllabus.
> **Structural Standard**: Every modern movement, conflict, and epoch is structured strictly across the **7-Step Analytical Framework**:
> `1. Background → 2. Causes → 3. Main Events / Programme / Participation → 4. Important Personalities → 5. British Response / Withdrawal → 6. Consequences → 7. Historical Significance`.
> **Editorial Rule**: Strictly point-based notes with maximum 25 words per point. Zero narrative paragraphs. Includes ASCII visual branch trees for every topic.

---

## 🏛️ The 7-Step Modern UPSC Analytical Framework

```text
1. BACKGROUND
   │
2. CAUSES
   ├── Political
   ├── Economic
   ├── Social
   └── Religious / Immediate
   │
3. MAIN EVENTS / PROGRAMME / PARTICIPATION
   ├── Programme of Action
   ├── Key Phases & Milestones
   └── Social Base & Mass Participation
   │
4. IMPORTANT PERSONALITIES
   │
5. BRITISH RESPONSE / WITHDRAWAL
   ├── Coercion & Repression
   ├── Legislation & Commissions
   └── Withdrawal Triggers
   │
6. CONSEQUENCES
   ├── Immediate Impacts
   ├── Institutional Transformations
   └── Limitations
   │
7. HISTORICAL SIGNIFICANCE
   ├── Ideological Legacy
   └── Constitutional & Sovereign Impact
```

---

# 1. Carnatic Wars & European Commercial Rivalry (1740–1763)

```text
CARNATIC WARS (1740–1763)
│
├── BACKGROUND
│   ├── Decline of Mughal central authority in Deccan
│   ├── Commercial rivalry between British and French East India Companies
│   ├── War of Austrian Succession in Europe
│   └── Disputed successions in Hyderabad and Carnatic
│
├── CAUSES
│   ├── Political: Imperial rivalries transplanted from Europe to India
│   ├── Economic: Monopolistic trade control over Coromandel textile exports
│   └── Strategic: Puppet nawab installations by Dupleix and Clive
│
├── MAIN EVENTS
│   ├── First Carnatic War (1746–1748): Battle of St. Thome
│   ├── Second Carnatic War (1749–1754): Battles of Ambur and Arcot
│   └── Third Carnatic War (1758–1763): Decisive Battle of Wandiwash
│
├── IMPORTANT PERSONALITIES
│   ├── Joseph Francois Dupleix, Robert Clive, Stringer Lawrence
│   └── Chanda Sahib, Anwar-ud-din, Nasir Jung, Muzaffar Jung
│
├── BRITISH RESPONSE
│   ├── Recall of Dupleix by French government (1754)
│   ├── British naval supremacy blockading French supplies
│   └── Complete destruction of French fortifications at Pondicherry
│
├── CONSEQUENCES
│   ├── Elimination of French political ambitions in India
│   ├── British East India Company established unchallenged coastal mastery
│   └── Carnatic and Hyderabad turned into British client dependencies
│
└── HISTORICAL SIGNIFICANCE
    ├── Proved small European-trained infantry could route large Indian armies
    └── Laid structural foundation for British territorial conquest of India
```

### 1. Background
- **Decline of Central Authority**: Death of Aurangzeb and Nizam-ul-Mulk's preoccupation weakened southern subahs.
- **European Commercial Enclaves**: French headquartered at Pondicherry; British East India Company anchored at Fort St. George (Madras).
- **Austrian Succession Spillover**: European hostilities between Great Britain and France triggered immediate naval clashes in Indian waters.
- **Coromandel Coast Trade**: High profits from cotton calicoes, silk, and spices incentivized exclusive imperial trade monopolies.

### 2. Causes
- **Political Causes**:
  - Absence of strong regional paramountcy enabled foreign companies to interfere in local dynastic disputes.
  - Dupleix's visionary ambition to convert a commercial corporation into an expansive territorial empire.
- **Economic Causes**:
  - Competing mercantilist desires to establish total monopoly over Coromandel weaving centers and ports.
  - Need to eliminate rival European tariff exemptions and customs privileges.
- **Immediate Causes**:
  - First War: British Royal Navy under Commodore Curtis Barnett seized French merchant ships (1745).
  - Second War: Simultaneous disputed successions to thrones of Hyderabad and Carnatic (1748).
  - Third War: Outbreak of the Seven Years' War in Europe (1756).

### 3. Main Events
- **First Carnatic War (1746–1748)**:
  - French captured Madras (1746) under Admiral La Bourdonnais and Governor Dupleix.
  - **Battle of St. Thome (1746)**: Tiny French force decisively defeated Nawab Anwar-ud-din's 10,000-strong cavalry army.
  - Concluded by **Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)**; Madras restored to British in exchange for Louisbourg (North America).
- **Second Carnatic War (1749–1754)**:
  - Dupleix backed Muzaffar Jung (Hyderabad) and Chanda Sahib (Carnatic).
  - British supported Nasir Jung (Hyderabad) and Muhammad Ali (Carnatic).
  - **Battle of Ambur (1749)**: Anwar-ud-din defeated and killed; Chanda Sahib became Nawab of Carnatic.
  - **Siege of Arcot (1751)**: Robert Clive captured and heroically defended Arcot for 53 days.
- **Third Carnatic War (1758–1763)**:
  - French dispatched Count de Lally; captured Fort St. David (1758).
  - Lally committed strategic blunder by recalling Bussy from Hyderabad court.
  - **Battle of Wandiwash (January 1760)**: British General Sir Eyre Coote crushed French forces under Lally.
  - British captured Pondicherry (1761), demolishing its fortifications.

### 4. Important Personalities
- **Joseph Francois Dupleix**: French Governor-General; pioneered subsidiary alliance tactics and European military intervention in Indian courts.
- **Robert Clive**: British clerk-turned-military officer; seized Arcot (1751), turning the strategic tide in favor of British.
- **Count de Lally**: French commander-in-chief; impetuous leadership and poor diplomacy alienated Indian allies and French officers.
- **Sir Eyre Coote**: British military commander; delivered decisive victory at Wandiwash (1760).
- **Charles Joseph Patissier, Marquis de Bussy**: Brilliant French officer who maintained French diplomatic control over the Nizam of Hyderabad.

### 5. British Response
- **Naval Superiority**: Deployed powerful Royal Navy squadrons to isolate French settlements and intercept reinforcements.
- **Financial Mobilization**: Leveraged vast commercial revenues from Bengal trade after 1757 to fund Carnatic military campaigns.
- **Diplomatic Neutralization**: Concluded **Treaty of Paris (1763)**; French factories returned but strictly forbidden from maintaining troops or fortifications.

### 6. Consequences
- **Elimination of French Power**: French East India Company reduced to mere commercial trading posts at Pondicherry, Chandernagore, and Mahe.
- **Puppet Carnatic Rulers**: Nawab Muhammad Ali made completely dependent on British financial loans and military protection.
- **Northern Circars Ceded**: British secured fertile coastal Northern Circars strip from the Nizam of Hyderabad.

### 7. Historical Significance
- **Military Revolution**: Exposed fatal vulnerability of traditional Indian feudal cavalry against disciplined European musket-firing infantry.
- **Imperial Blueprint**: Provided British East India Company the master operational strategy used subsequently to conquer Bengal, Mysore, and Marathas.

---

# 2. British Conquest of Bengal: Plassey (1757) to Buxar (1764) & Dual Government

```text
CONQUEST OF BENGAL (1757–1772)
│
├── BACKGROUND
│   ├── Bengal as richest Mughal Subah (contributed 50% of Asian trade imports)
│   ├── Farrukhsiyar's Farman of 1717 granting Dastak privileges
│   └── Nawab Siraj-ud-Daulah's accession and defiance of Company encroachments
│
├── CAUSES
│   ├── Political: Unauthorised fortification of Fort William by British
│   ├── Economic: Rampant misuse of private trade Dastaks by Company officials
│   └── Immediate: Black Hole Tragedy allegations and refuge to fugitive Krishna Das
│
├── MAIN EVENTS
│   ├── Treaty of Alinagar (February 1757)
│   ├── Battle of Plassey (23 June 1757): Betrayal by Mir Jafar
│   ├── Deposition of Mir Jafar and accession of Mir Qasim (1760)
│   ├── Battle of Buxar (22 October 1764): Triple Alliance routed
│   └── Treaty of Allahabad (1765): Diwani rights granted to Company
│
├── IMPORTANT PERSONALITIES
│   ├── Siraj-ud-Daulah, Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim
│   ├── Robert Clive, Major Hector Munro
│   └── Jagat Seth (Fateh Chand), Omichund, Rai Durlabh
│
├── BRITISH RESPONSE
│   ├── Installation of compliant puppet Nawabs (Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim, Najm-ud-Daulah)
│   ├── Institution of Robert Clive's Dual Government (1765–1772)
│   └── Direct annexation and abolition of Dual System by Warren Hastings (1772)
│
├── CONSEQUENCES
│   ├── Complete economic plunder and Bengal Famine of 1770
│   ├── Company transformed from merchant trader to sovereign territorial master
│   └── Bengal revenues financed subsequent British military expansions across India
│
└── HISTORICAL SIGNIFICANCE
    ├── Buxar made British de facto masters of Northern India up to Awadh
    └── Laid economic bedrock of British colonial empire in Asia
```

### 1. Background
- **Economic Pre-eminence**: Bengal accounted for nearly 60% of British East India Company imports from Asia (saltpeter, silk, calico).
- **Imperial Farman of 1717**: Emperor Farrukhsiyar granted duty-free trade rights against annual payment of Rs. 3,000.
- **Sovereign Friction**: Successive Nawabs (Murshid Quli Khan, Alivardi Khan) resisted Company attempts to subvert provincial customs revenues.
- **Young Nawab's Accession (1756)**: Siraj-ud-Daulah succeeded Alivardi Khan; faced treacherous court intrigues from Ghaseti Begum and Shaukat Jung.

### 2. Causes
- **Political Causes**:
  - British gave asylum to political fugitive Krishna Das, son of corrupt governor Raj Ballabh.
  - British insulted the sovereign Nawab by refusing to dismantle illegal fortifications at Fort William (Calcutta).
- **Economic Causes**:
  - British private merchants rampantly misused duty-free imperial trade permits (**Dastaks**), defrauding provincial treasury.
  - Indian merchants suffered unfair competition, leading to widespread loss of internal trade revenue.
- **Immediate Causes**:
  - Siraj-ud-Daulah attacked and seized Calcutta (June 1756); renamed it **Alinagar**.
  - Exaggerated British propaganda of **Black Hole Tragedy** (Holwell claimed 123 soldiers died of suffocation).

### 3. Main Events
- **Battle of Plassey (23 June 1757)**:
  - Clive forged secret conspiracy with Commander-in-Chief Mir Jafar, financier Jagat Seth, and Omichund.
  - Battle fought on banks of Bhagirathi River; Mir Jafar and Rai Durlabh kept their armies inactive.
  - Siraj-ud-Daulah fled; captured and assassinated by Mir Jafar's son Miran.
- **Reign of Mir Qasim (1760–1763)**:
  - Mir Qasim replaced Mir Jafar (1760); ceded Burdwan, Midnapore, and Chittagong districts to Company.
  - Shifted capital from Murshidabad to **Munger** to escape British surveillance.
  - Modernized army with European guns and abolished all internal transit customs to level playing field for Indian traders.
- **Battle of Buxar (22 October 1764)**:
  - Mir Qasim formed **Triple Alliance** with Nawab Shuja-ud-Daulah of Awadh and Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II.
  - Major **Hector Munro** commanded disciplined British force; decisively routed the allied Indian army at Buxar (Bihar).
- **Treaty of Allahabad (August 1765)**:
  - Clive secured **Diwani Rights** (revenue collection of Bengal, Bihar, Orissa) from Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II.
  - Emperor granted annual tribute of Rs. 26 lakhs and districts of Kara and Manikpur.
  - Nawab of Awadh forced to pay Rs. 50 lakh war indemnity; Awadh converted into a buffer state.

### 4. Important Personalities
- **Siraj-ud-Daulah**: Last independent Nawab of Bengal; defeated at Plassey due to military betrayal by his generals.
- **Mir Jafar**: Commander who betrayed Siraj-ud-Daulah; dubbed "Clive's Jackal" for paying enormous personal bribes to Company officials.
- **Mir Qasim**: Competent administrator who attempted to modernize revenue and military systems before rebelling against Company exploitation.
- **Robert Clive**: Architect of British political supremacy in Bengal; instituted Dual Government (1765).
- **Jagat Seth (Mahtab Rai)**: Ultra-wealthy imperial banker of Bengal; funded British conspiracy to depose Siraj-ud-Daulah.

### 5. British Response
- **Establishment of Dual Government (1765–1772)**:
  - **Diwani** (fiscal and civil justice) held directly by the Company.
  - **Nizamat** (police, criminal justice, and civil administration) left to Nawab through Deputy Nazim.
  - Company wielded complete power without any administrative responsibility; Nawab held nominal responsibility without financial resources.
- **Abolition by Warren Hastings (1772)**:
  - Terminated Dual Government; dismissed Deputy Diwans Mohammad Reza Khan and Raja Shitab Rai; Company took direct governance.

### 6. Consequences
- **Economic Destruction & Drain of Wealth**:
  - Bengal revenues used to buy Indian silk and textiles ("investment") without importing bullion from Britain.
  - Artisans coerced into signing monopolistic supply contracts under Company agents (**Gumashtas**).
- **Catastrophic Bengal Famine (1770)**:
  - Over 10 million people (one-third of Bengal's population) perished while Company ruthlessly enforced full revenue collections.

### 7. Historical Significance
- **Plassey vs. Buxar Distinction**:
  - Plassey was a skirmish won by treason and bribery; Buxar was a pitched military contest proving British operational superiority.
  - Transformed East India Company from a commercial mercantile body into the sovereign ruler of India's richest province.

---

# 3. Anglo-Mysore Wars: Resistance of Hyder Ali & Tipu Sultan (1767–1799)

```text
ANGLO-MYSORE WARS (1767–1799)
│
├── BACKGROUND
│   ├── Rise of Mysore under Hyder Ali (1761) over Wodeyar dynasty
│   ├── Strategic location controlling Malabar spice trade and Deccan routes
│   └── Modernized artillery and French military alliance
│
├── CAUSES
│   ├── Territorial clashes over Northern Circars and Carnatic frontiers
│   ├── British dread of French-Mysorean military collaboration
│   └── Mysore's economic embargo on British pepper and sandalwood exports
│
├── MAIN EVENTS
│   ├── First War (1767–1769): Hyder Ali dictated Treaty of Madras
│   ├── Second War (1780–1784): Battle of Pollilur; Treaty of Mangalore
│   ├── Third War (1790–1792): Cornwallis's siege; Treaty of Seringapatam
│   └── Fourth War (1799): Fall of Seringapatam; heroic death of Tipu Sultan
│
├── IMPORTANT PERSONALITIES
│   ├── Hyder Ali, Tipu Sultan ("Tiger of Mysore")
│   └── Warren Hastings, Lord Cornwallis, Lord Wellesley, General Harris
│
├── BRITISH RESPONSE
│   ├── Forged triple alliances uniting British, Marathas, and the Nizam
│   ├── Systematic economic strangulation and territorial annexation
│   └── Imposed Subsidiary Alliance on restored infant Wodeyar Raja (1799)
│
├── CONSEQUENCES
│   ├── Partition of Mysore kingdom among British, Nizam, and Marathas
│   ├── Complete elimination of French influence from southern India
│   └── Total British hegemony established over South Indian peninsula
│
└── HISTORICAL SIGNIFICANCE
    ├── Fiercest state resistance encountered by the British in 18th century
    └── Pioneered rocket artillery and modern state mercantilism in India
```

### 1. Background
- **Rise of Hyder Ali (1761)**: Rose from soldier to de facto ruler, eclipsing Prime Minister Nanjaraja and titular Wodeyar kings.
- **Strategic Power**: Established modern artillery arsenal at Dindigul with French technicians; built disciplined infantry and cavalry.
- **Maritime Hegemony**: Mysore controlled Malabar ports (Calicut, Cannanore, Mangalore), dominating rich spice, timber, and horse trades.
- **Deccan Geopolitics**: Mysore was locked in continuous tripartite frontier warfare with the Marathas and the Nizam of Hyderabad.

### 2. Causes
- **Political Causes**:
  - British feared Mysore's rapid expansion towards Carnatic frontiers and Madras borders.
  - Hyder Ali and Tipu Sultan cultivated close military and diplomatic ties with Revolutionary France.
- **Economic Causes**:
  - Tipu banned export of sandalwood, pepper, and cardamom through Mysore ports, choking British trade monopolies.
  - Tipu established state commercial corporations, modern shipyards, and attempted direct trade with Oman and Ottoman Turkey.
- **Immediate Causes**:
  - First War: British joined offensive alliance with Nizam and Marathas against Hyder Ali (1766).
  - Second War: British captured French port of **Mahe** located within Hyder Ali's sovereign jurisdiction (1779).
  - Third War: Tipu attacked Raja of Travancore, a key British ally, over fortification disputes (1789).
  - Fourth War: Tipu planted "Tree of Liberty" at Seringapatam and joined the French Jacobin Club; Wellesley demanded unconditional submission.

### 3. Main Events
- **First Anglo-Mysore War (1767–1769)**:
  - Hyder Ali outmaneuvered British-Nizam coalition; detached Nizam through astute diplomacy.
  - Marched within 5 miles of Madras; forced British to sign humiliating defensive **Treaty of Madras (1769)**.
- **Second Anglo-Mysore War (1780–1784)**:
  - Hyder captured Arcot and crushed Colonel Baillie's force at **Battle of Pollilur (1780)**.
  - Sir Eyre Coote checked Hyder at **Porto Novo (1781)**.
  - Hyder Ali died of cancer (1782); his son Tipu Sultan continued the war.
  - Concluded on terms of mutual restitution under **Treaty of Mangalore (1784)**.
- **Third Anglo-Mysore War (1790–1792)**:
  - Lord Cornwallis allied with Marathas and Nizam; besieged Tipu inside capital Seringapatam.
  - **Treaty of Seringapatam (1792)**: Tipu surrendered half his territory; paid Rs. 3.3 crore indemnity and surrendered two sons as hostages.
- **Fourth Anglo-Mysore War (1799)**:
  - Lord Wellesley launched coordinated double offensive under General Harris and General Stuart.
  - Seringapatam was breached on **4 May 1799**; Tipu Sultan died fighting heroically at the city water gate.

### 4. Important Personalities
- **Hyder Ali**: Brilliant military strategist and diplomat; only Indian ruler to dictate a peace treaty to the British at Madras.
- **Tipu Sultan**: "Tiger of Mysore"; introduced solar calendar, new coinage, state trading corporation, and pioneer military Mysorean rockets.
- **Lord Cornwallis**: Governor-General who personally commanded campaigns in the Third Mysore War, crippling Tipu's strategic capacity.
- **Lord Wellesley**: Imperialist Governor-General who utilized Subsidiary Alliance system to wage total war against Tipu Sultan.

### 5. British Response
- **Coalition Warfare**: Consistently appeased and allied with Marathas and Nizam to isolate Mysore from Indian regional support.
- **Territorial Disruption**: Ceded northern Mysore territories to Nizam and Marathas while British seized strategic Kanara, Coimbatore, and Wayanad.
- **Restoration of Puppet Wodeyars**: Placed 5-year-old Krishnaraja III on Mysore throne under a suffocating **Subsidiary Alliance (1799)**.

### 6. Consequences
- **Mysore Partitioned**: Core territories became landlocked princely state controlled by British Resident; rest annexed directly by Madras Presidency.
- **French Eradication**: Decisively severed French diplomatic channel to India, removing Napoleon's prospective eastern invasion route.
- **Unchallenged Southern Dominion**: British secured uncontested territorial mastery across entire South Indian peninsula.

### 7. Historical Significance
- **Modernized Resistance**: Tipu pioneered state-directed industrialism, military rocket artillery (**Mysorean rockets**), and independent naval docks.
- **Turning Point**: Elimination of Mysore removed the only southern Indian state capable of defeating European regular armies in open battle.

---

# 4. Anglo-Maratha Wars & Fall of the Maratha Confederacy (1775–1818)

```text
ANGLO-MARATHA WARS (1775–1818)
│
├── BACKGROUND
│   ├── Third Battle of Panipat (1761) checked northward Maratha expansion
│   ├── Decentralized Maratha Confederacy: Peshwa, Scindia, Holkar, Gaekwad, Bhonsle
│   └── Succession crises in Poona court following Peshwa Madhavrao's death (1772)
│
├── CAUSES
│   ├── Political: British ambition to control Bombay hinterland and install puppet Peshwas
│   ├── Internal Feuds: Raghunath Rao (Raghoba) sought British military aid
│   └── Wellesley's Aggressive Imperialism: Enforcement of Subsidiary Alliance
│
├── MAIN EVENTS
│   ├── First War (1775–1782): Battle of Wadgaon; Treaty of Salbai (1782)
│   ├── Second War (1803–1805): Treaty of Bassein; Battles of Assaye and Laswari
│   └── Third War (1817–1818): Battles of Khadki, Koregaon; Pindari operations
│
├── IMPORTANT PERSONALITIES
│   ├── Nana Phadnavis ("Maratha Machiavelli"), Mahadji Scindia, Yashwantrao Holkar
│   ├── Peshwa Baji Rao II, Raghunath Rao
│   └── Warren Hastings, Lord Wellesley, Lord Hastings (Moira), Arthur Wellesley
│
├── BRITISH RESPONSE
│   ├── Signed conflicting treaties from Bombay and Calcutta Councils
│   ├── Imposed Subsidiary Alliance on Peshwa through Treaty of Bassein (1802)
│   └── Abolished the office of Peshwa; annexed core Maratha territories
│
├── CONSEQUENCES
│   ├── Dismantling of the Maratha Confederacy
│   ├── Peshwa Baji Rao II exiled to Bithur on annual pension
│   └── British East India Company became undisputed paramount power of India
│
└── HISTORICAL SIGNIFICANCE
    ├── Ended the only Pan-Indian indigenous successor state to the Mughal Empire
    └── Established complete Pax Britannica across Central India, Deccan, and Gujarat
```

### 1. Background
- **Post-Panipat Rebound**: Mahadji Scindia and Peshwa Madhavrao quickly recovered Maratha power, restoring Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II to Delhi (1771).
- **Structure of Confederacy**: Autonomous regional warlords operated under nominal leadership of the Pune-based Peshwa:
  - **Peshwa** at Pune; **Scindia** at Gwalior; **Holkar** at Indore; **Bhonsle** at Nagpur; **Gaekwad** at Baroda.
- **Poona Court Factionalism**: Assassination of young Peshwa Narayanrao (1773) triggered bitter civil war between Raghunath Rao and Nana Phadnavis.
- **Bombay Presidency's Ambition**: British Bombay sought commercial revenue parity with wealthy Bengal through territorial conquests in Salsette and Bassein.

### 2. Causes
- **Political Causes**:
  - Raghunath Rao fled to Bombay British, signing treacherous **Treaty of Surat (1775)** to gain Peshwaship.
  - Nana Phadnavis rejected Treaty of Surat, backing infant posthumous Peshwa Madhavrao Narayan (Twelve Bhai Council).
- **Imperialistic Causes**:
  - Lord Wellesley's aggressive forward policy to eliminate Maratha neutrality and force them into Subsidiary Alliances.
- **Immediate Causes**:
  - First War: Bombay government dispatched military expedition to impose Raghunath Rao on Poona (1775).
  - Second War: Yashwantrao Holkar routed combined forces of Peshwa and Scindia at Hadapsar (1802); cowardly Baji Rao II signed **Treaty of Bassein**.
  - Third War: Lord Hastings encircled Maratha territories under pretext of suppressing roving **Pindari** freebooters (1817).

### 3. Main Events
- **First Anglo-Maratha War (1775–1782)**:
  - Marathas lured British army into Western Ghats passes; forced surrender at **Battle of Wadgaon (1779)**.
  - Warren Hastings dispatched Colonel Goddard (captured Ahmedabad) and Captain Popham (captured Gwalior Fort).
  - Mahadji Scindia negotiated **Treaty of Salbai (May 1782)**: Guaranteed 20 years of Anglo-Maratha peace; British retained Salsette.
- **Second Anglo-Maratha War (1803–1805)**:
  - **Treaty of Bassein (1802)**: Peshwa Baji Rao II sold Maratha sovereignty for British protection.
  - Scindia and Bhonsle rejected treaty; launched war against British.
  - Major-General Arthur Wellesley crushed Scindia-Bhonsle forces at **Battle of Assaye (1803)**.
  - Lord Lake captured Delhi and Agra, winning **Battle of Laswari (1803)**.
  - Forced treaties: **Deogaon** (with Bhonsle) and **Surji-Anjangaon** (with Scindia).
- **Third Anglo-Maratha War (1817–1818)**:
  - Peshwa Baji Rao II made desperate bid for freedom; burned British Residency at Pune.
  - British defeated Peshwa at **Battle of Khadki** and **Battle of Koregaon (1818)**.
  - Scindia compelled to remain neutral by **Treaty of Gwalior (1817)**.
  - Holkar defeated at **Battle of Mahidpur (1817)**; signed **Treaty of Mandasor (1818)**.

### 4. Important Personalities
- **Nana Phadnavis**: Chief Minister at Pune; held Maratha Confederacy together through astute diplomatic equilibrium until death (1800).
- **Mahadji Scindia**: Ruler of Gwalior; raised modernized infantry trained by French general Benoit de Boigne; controlled Delhi Emperor.
- **Peshwa Baji Rao II**: Incompetent and treacherous ruler whose egoistic infighting destroyed Maratha unity and invited British subjugation.
- **Arthur Wellesley (Duke of Wellington)**: Commanded southern army; perfected combined arms tactics at Battle of Assaye.
- **Lord Hastings (Marquess of Hastings)**: Governor-General who planned comprehensive encirclement and final liquidation of Maratha power.

### 5. British Response
- **Imposition of Subsidiary Alliances**: Disarmed each Maratha chief separately, stationing permanent British subsidiary forces at state capitals.
- **Abolition of the Peshwaship (1818)**: Liquidated hereditary office of Peshwa; exiled Baji Rao II to Bithur near Kanpur.
